HTTPS (D79, #119) merged into master while this was a draft; its routes have their rows above, in `policy.OUTSIDE` and in `tests/test_roles.py`. The server answers them itself (`_https_route`), and each asks `Api.gate` through `_who` before it is looked at, so an Operator gains none of them; a path under `/api/https/` that has no row asks for an Owner. The probe alone asks nobody (`policy.NO_GATE`). D79's switch "Owner access only over the secure connection" keeps `full` devices off plain http and says of a code that it "gives view or live access, over HTTP, as before"; neither decision asks more for an Operator, so an Operator still works over plain http while the switch is on. He is now close to an owner: an open question for the owner.

**The panel, as built** (#120). The three names come from `roleName`; the box tells the panel its reach in one word (`GET /api/status`, `reach`: owner, operator, presenter, guest, watch) so the page never works it out by itself. An Operator gets Edit pads, upload, rename and delete on Media, Schedule, At power-up, Projection mapping, the room's set-up, shader and effect presets, sets, uploads and detail, a card Guests (guest devices to remove, a guest link) and the lock (a card on Room and on People and codes). A Guest gets one card of his own, "Guest controls", on Live and Room: previous, next, stop, blackout, a clip, the scenes that play no stream, ambience, a shader, projectors on and off (off asks, then sends the box's token); the pages' own controls stay disabled for a guest as before, so nothing he can press is refused; locked, the card is one line. **Not in the panel yet, though the box allows it:** for a Guest, effects, shader presets, one projector's input and one group; for an Operator, the Look page (choosing and exporting a look), the Schedule's own on and off switch (it shares a switch with the module, which is the Owner's), restarting the player.

**The panel in the four areas** (D72, master with #121, merged into this branch on 2026-10-10; this replaces where the paragraph above says Live, Room and System). The lock, `guestLockCard`, is a card on Room > Guests and on Setup > People and codes. A guest's own card (`guestCard`, or the one line while locked) is the first thing on Room > Scenes, where a guest lands; on a box without the Room it is the first thing on Play > Pads; it is never on the page twice. Stop, Blackout, Previous and Next are not in that card any more: they are the strip's own buttons, enabled for a guest while guest controls are open and sent as his forms (`transport()`, `guestSend`); every other control of the strip stays disabled for him, and all of them while locked. A change of the lock draws a guest's panel again at his next poll. The Operator has the pages Setup > Room (groups and scenes: it was the Owner's alone in the new layout), Schedule, At power-up and MIDI controller, and Mapping as a page of its own without the module's switch, which stays the Owner's. Decided here, not asked: a guest's Previous and Next are in the strip too (the box takes them from a guest), and the guest's card lost its own four buttons rather than keep a second copy.

**Nothing twice, and nothing a guest sees is refused** (the coordinator's rule after the first carry, 2026-10-11; this replaces what the paragraph above says of the card's place and contents). For a guest, a screen's own buttons are his where the box takes the action from him, sent as his form through `guestSend`: the scene buttons of Room > Scenes (the scenes the box lists for him, without the ones that play a stream), the pads, Play on a clip in the Library, the big Vibes button on Pads, and Stop, Blackout, Previous and Next in the strip. His card holds only what has no other place for him: a shader to show, the next one, the projectors, and on Room the ambience's start and stop (the Room's ambience card only says what plays to a guest); it stands under the scenes. Chosen over hiding the normal list and keeping a second one in the card: one place for a scene, for everybody. Locked, the scene card is not shown and the line "The room is locked for a show: you can watch" stands first. What stays disabled for a guest is what was never his (the picture, the sound, the fades, the transition).

**The re-check of #120 at `bee3af6`** (2026-10-11: the six Lows fixed, no High or Medium, three new Lows, each fixed with a test in `SecondReviewTest` that failed first). (1) An Operator could put a mapping of his own in front of the Owner's access-code control: a mapping for one controller or one channel is matched before one for any, so the Owner's pad went quiet with his entry untouched. For anyone but the Owner, a new or changed mapping is now refused where its kind and number meet one of the Owner's mappings (for any controller, on any channel) or a control of a standard layout that shows an access code, in a layer too; Learn ends in the same request, and a settings file is the Owner's to load. (2) "A sender must be a paired device" (D78) counted a presenter paired from a controller by his stored role; it asks `Auth.role_of` now, so he is a Guest there as everywhere. (3) A removal asked by a device that is itself gone (a reset or a removal while the request was on its way) removes nobody. **Noted, no code:** an Operator may switch MIDI off (`POST /api/midi {"enabled": false}`), which follows from the owner's answer that Operators edit the controllers; and the reviewer holds that "Owner access only over the secure connection" not covering Operators is acceptable for now and could become a second switch later.

**Known and left as they are** (the independent review of #120 at `571d2ac`, which found no High or Medium; its six Lows are fixed, each with a test in `ReviewTest`). A confirm token is handed out for a power-off whose target does not exist (the second request then answers 404 and nothing is counted). A token sent with a different body is used up (the guest taps again).

**An open question the review adds: scenes for guests.** A guest may apply every scene that plays no stream, so what a scene does (mutes, a power-off after the confirm) is what a guest can do. The reviewer holds that a mark on each scene, "guests may use", set by an Operator, is safer than "all non-stream scenes". Answered by the owner on 2026-10-10 and built: see "The owner's answers" below.

**The owner's answers, 2026-10-10, and what was built for each** (these replace what is said differently above; tests: `OwnerAnswersTest`, `HeldBackTest`, `PeopleTest` in `tests/test_roles.py`).

- "1. Yes keep stop, blackout and effects for Guests". Kept, with the review's cooldown on blackout.
- "2. Keep, code on screen from midi controller defaults to Guest. Allow for switching to Operators, especially if Guests are locked out." One setting, an Owner's only, with the other controller-code switches: `POST /api/access/controller {"join": "view" | "live"}`, kept as `controller_code.join` and only when it is `live` (an older release drops the key and pairs what it always paired). Set to Operator, the join code pairs a `live` device with an Operator's full reach (its record carries `"join": "live"`), the display says "One-time operator code", and the panel says once beside the choice that anyone at the controller can then become an Operator. The box never changes it by itself: locking guest controls does not; where the Owner sees the lock closed, one line points at the setting. A code on the display when the choice changes is ended. Devices paired under either choice stay what they were. Every device from a controller code that is not an Owner still goes after 7 unused days.
- "3. Old presenters are moved up to Guests." A `live` device that was paired from a controller's code before this change is a Guest: `Auth.role_of`, the one place a stored role is read for a request and for the device list. Exactly a Guest's reach, the lock included, and named Guest in People and codes. The record is not rewritten and there is no schema change: an older release reads the same file and gives the device what it had, not more (tried with master's code). A presenter the Owner invited by a link or a code is an Operator. `policy.LEGACY_LIVE` now holds only the controllers, the schedule and support sessions below `full`. An Owner can invite such a phone as an Operator.
- "4. Operators are the daily users and should have midi controller editing permissions." `POST /api/midi`, `/api/midi/learn`, `/api/midi/map` and `/api/midi/lights` are the Operator's. Enforced in the handlers, because the objection of the review stands: an Operator may not set, change or remove a control whose action is the Owner's. Those are the actions an Operator could not perform himself: today the two that draw an access code on the display (`code_join`, `code_owner`; in the code, every MIDI action of the kind "hold"). For an Operator the Owner's mappings must be the same before and after any change to the map; so a posted mapping, what Learn found, a new action on a drawn control, a mapping on the same note (it would take the Owner's place), Remove, and Reset are refused when they touch one, and his "Remove all" leaves the Owner's. He cannot give the layout's own code pad another action, and cannot switch the standard layout of a controller whose layout has such a pad, or of a controller that is not plugged in (decided here: switching it adds or removes that control). There is no route that takes a whole map; a settings file is the Owner's to load. He sees the Owner's mappings and cannot change them. What any mapping does runs as `midi`, held to the list of before D80, so a mapped control can never do more than a presenter could, which is less than an Operator. The module's switch and the controller-code switches stay the Owner's. (Decided here: `code_join` counts as the Owner's too, since with the Owner's choice it pairs an Operator.)
- "5. Guests can play all scenes, except when marked not for guest use or something." A scene may carry `"no_guests": true` ("Not for guests" in the scene's editor, set by an Operator or Owner; kept only when set, so every scene made before is as it was, and master's `room.validate` reads such a scene and leaves the key out). A guest is refused such a scene however he names it (id, number, name), no confirm is offered for it, and `GET /api/room` does not list it for a guest. A scene that plays a stream stays refused, marked or not.
- "Operators can remove other operators just not themselves or owners." `POST /api/devices/revoke` by an Operator: a Guest or another Operator; himself 403 (he leaves with Log out); an Owner 403. Only a device in the list can be named, so never a controller, a support session or one of the box's own callers. `GET /api/devices` gives an Operator the guests and the operators. The removed device's token is dead at once.
- "Yes guest controls are unlocked by default. Existing guests gain new controls." As built.

**Where the first build differed from the brief of 2026-10-10** (all answered since: see "The owner's answers" below)**.** (1) A Guest also gets Stop, Blackout and effects on and off; the brief named scene, ambience, shader or preset, pad or clip, next and previous, projectors. (2) MIDI layout, Learn and lights stay the Owner's. (3) The box still saves a guest device's last-used day; the brief said a guest writes nothing. (4) The controllers' list is one list for all of them (what a presenter could reach before), not one per controller: each controller already sends only what its own module maps. (5) A presenter paired from a controller before the update is held to the presenter's old reach. (6) A guest may apply a scene that mutes, though he may not mute directly.

**Nothing of this has run on a box**, with a phone, a projector or a controller. Rows are in `tools/DEVICE-TESTING.md`.

## D81. Two ways back in for an owner who lost every device: printed recovery codes, and a USB stick at the box

2026-10-10. The owner's words: "I do need a reliable and user friendly way in for an owner to in the event codes are forgotten and devices are lost etc." Offered two additions, he said "Build both". (D80 is taken by the open roles branch.) Until now, with every owner device lost, the ways in were `sudo pvj-pin` at a keyboard or over SSH, the first-run PIN screen (only once every device is removed, which needs an owner), or a factory reset.

**The threat model, written before the code.** The box sits on a venue network. Who can try what:

- *A stranger on the network, nobody present.* Today the PIN is four digits and lives until the next restart, behind a throttle (5 wrong per address per minute, 20 box-wide per 10 minutes, then 1 and 5 minute locks). A recovery code is worse than the PIN in two ways: it does not change at restart, and it works without anyone at the box. So its strength must make guessing hopeless at the throttle's rate and offline against the settings file: 12 symbols from a 31 symbol alphabet is about 59 bits; at the throttle's best (a few hundred guesses an hour, against at most 8 hashes) that is never, and offline against scrypt (n 2^14) from a stolen card it is thousands of years, where the card already holds everything. A wrong code must leave nothing by its timing, and a box without codes must answer exactly like a box with them.
- *A stranger who floods the pairing route to keep the real owner out.* Shared with the PIN: the box-wide lock is 5 minutes and the counter starts again after it, so a flood of addresses can keep pairing locked, as it can today for the PIN (SECURITY.md, "Not covered"). The stick ends that: putting it in lifts the lock (presence at the box outranks a stranger on the network).
- *A stranger with the owner's cookie or a stolen operator device.* May never make, see or cancel codes (full access only, and the roles branch's Operator never gets `full` for these routes); never through remote support; never over plain http while the owner chose "Owner access only over the secure connection".
- *Someone in the room when a PIN is on the projector.* The screen is the room's projector: the digits are public for two minutes. So the PIN shown for the stick is a fresh one, and it dies at its first use, at two minutes, when the stick is pulled or when the switch goes off: whoever read it off the wall has nothing afterwards.
- *Someone who can touch the box.* Can pull the card and read everything; the stick gives them a PIN they could already take. In a venue where strangers reach the box the owner turns the stick way off; the manual says so.
- *Someone who finds the paper.* Has owner access until the owner cancels the set; the panel says where to keep it and shows every use with a name and a time.
- *The settings file, an export, the journal, a diagnostics file.* Carry hashes at most, never a code; an export carries no hash at all (like `auth` and `devices`).

**Recovery codes, what was decided.**

- **Form:** 8 codes per set, each `XXXX-XXXX-XXXX` from `ABCDEFGHJKMNPQRSTUVWXYZ23456789` (no 0, O, 1, I, L), made with `secrets.choice`. Typed case-blind, spaces and dashes ignored (`auth.normalise_recovery`). About 59 bits each.
- **Stored** in `settings["recovery"]["codes"]` as one 16 byte salt per set and one scrypt hash per code (the PIN's parameters), with when and by whom the set was made. Shown once, in the answer that makes them, and never again: no `GET` has them, no log line, the diagnostics file names the count only, an export never has the section's hashes. A new set replaces the old; Cancel removes all.
- **Redeemed through `POST /api/pair`**, the code in the `pin` field: one route, one lock (`_pair_lock`), one throttle. A value that normalises to 12 alphabet symbols takes the recovery branch; anything else is as before. One scrypt per attempt whether a set exists or not (a fixed salt when none) and every hash compared with `hmac.compare_digest`, then the same words for a wrong code and for no set: "wrong or used recovery code". A wrong code is a fail of the shared throttle, exactly like a wrong PIN. Burning the code and adding the device is one save under `settings.lock`; if the device cannot be added (the list is full, the disk refused) the code is put back, so two devices racing one code get exactly one winner and a failed pairing costs no code.
- **What it pairs:** a full-access device, `via: "recovery"`, counted like a PIN pairing (in the reserved places, D48). The box writes one journal line with the device name, the address and how many codes are left (never the code), keeps an event in `recovery.log` (the last 20, no secrets) and People and codes shows every owner "A recovery code was used by X at T; N left". The PIN is not revealed; the new owner reads it under People and codes as any owner (D77).
- **Who may make or cancel:** `full` only (`GET /api/recovery`, `POST /api/recovery/codes`, `/api/recovery/cancel`, `/api/recovery/usb`), refused for a device removed meanwhile (`_still_paired`), never through remote support (`/api/recovery` is in `support.REMOTE_DENY_PREFIX`), and while the owner-only switch of D79 is on, only over TLS (the existing `owner_gate` covers every full route; tested for these).
- **Redeeming over plain http while the owner-only switch is on: refused**, with the https address in the answer, as the PIN is (the server's guard already refuses everything but a six digit code over http then, and takes back a full device made over http). Weighed: a lost-device owner with a new phone has no root installed, so https shows a warning he must click through; against that, a recovery code is worth more than the PIN, it crosses the venue's network once and gives everything, and the owner chose the switch on a device with the root. The pairing screen says it: "This box takes owner codes over https:// only; open <address>, accept the browser's warning if this phone has not got the root." The console remains (`sudo pvj-pin`, or `sudo rm -r /var/lib/pvj/tls`).
- **Backups:** the hashes are not in an export and not taken from one (`recovery` is exported and imported with its `usb` switch only). An export is a file that gets mailed and kept; a set belongs to one box, like its PIN and devices. A rebuilt box has no device, so it draws its PIN on the first-run screen and the owner makes a new set. A factory reset removes the section (`_wipe_access` starts from the defaults).
- **No schema change.** `recovery` is a key an older release keeps and ignores: master's `Settings.load` keeps unknown keys and writes them back, and master's `pair()` treats a 12 character code as a wrong PIN. First tried by hand, then made a test: `test_rollback_...` unpacks the release before this branch (`b62f203`, `git archive`) and runs its own `Settings` and `Auth` in a subprocess on a settings file written here; it loads, the section survives two new PINs, both forms of a code get "wrong PIN", and back on this release the same file's codes still work. The test skips where the clone is shallow (CI's checkout), so it is the Mac's proof. Master's import refuses a file with a section it does not know, as it does for every newer file.

**The USB stick, what was decided.**

- **The trigger:** a regular file named `pvj-recover` or `pvj-recover.txt` (case-blind) at the top level of a USB drive the box mounted (the existing mounter, `pvj/usb.py`: `/media/pvj/<label>`, vfat, exfat, ext2 to 4 or ntfs, read-only, `noexec`). Only the name and the kind are looked at (`lstat`: never a symlink, never a folder, never opened or read). Nothing on a stick is ever executed or parsed. A stick with clips and the file is both: the clips are listed for import as before, the file is not a clip and never was listed.
- **When:** the PIN screen's tick (every 3 s) asks `Recovery.poll()`; it fires on the change from absent to present, so once per insertion, and once at boot when a stick is in. At most 6 times an hour (`STICK_PER_HOUR`); beyond that, and while the switch is off, the insertion is logged and nothing is shown.
- **What happens:** a fresh PIN (`auth.rotate_pin`, which also lifts any lockout), drawn by the player over whatever is playing with the panel address, "works once" and "take the stick out to hide it now", for 120 s (`STICK_SECONDS`). It ends at the first pairing with it, at 120 s, when the stick is pulled (within about a second: while the PIN shows, the stick's scan thread looks every second and ends it itself, display included; the screen's 3 s tick is the fallback), or when the switch is turned off; at the end the PIN is rotated again, so the digits are dead. The journal gets a line at the start and the end (never the PIN), owners see "The PIN was shown on the box's screen at T because a recovery stick was put in" on People and codes, and snapshots for devices without full access leave the text out while it shows (`access_on_screen`).
- **The switch** `recovery.usb`, on by default (he asked for a way in that needs no preparation), owner-only, in the export, and the manual says what remains with it off.
- **It needs no paired device, no network, no working panel session**: it lives in the panel process beside the PIN screen, writes only the settings file (as every new PIN does, so the read-only root is no obstacle), and a player that is restarting is simply drawn to at the next tick while the PIN stays valid for its window.

**The throttle for a PIN shown to a room.** Four digits for two minutes: at the throttle's rate a stranger in the room who starts guessing gets about 20 tries before the box-wide lock, 0.2 percent; the owner who typed it has a fresh PIN behind him the moment he is in. The first-run screen today shows a PIN that lives until restart; this one is shorter lived.

**The panel.** People and codes gains a Recovery card for owners: Make recovery codes (asks first when a set exists), the codes once in a form to print or copy, how many are left and when the set was made, Cancel all, the stick switch, and the notices. The pairing screen gains "I have a recovery code" and a "Locked out?" help naming both ways and the factory reset. Two small choices there: a typed code holding 0, O, 1, I or L is stopped on the page ("Read the paper again"), since the alphabet has none and the box would count it as a wrong guess against the throttle; and a refusal over plain http while the owner-only switch is on is said in the page's own words with the https address. The journal line for a redeemed code is handed from `pair()` to the API per device id (`recovery_uses`), not through one shared slot, so two devices redeeming two codes at once both get their line.

**After the independent security review (2026-10-11, nothing High; two Medium, five Low, all fixed, each with a test seen to fail first).** M1: a use of the stick's PIN leaves a durable trace: the device is tagged `via: "stick"` (`auth.mark_via`), the log gets `stick-used` with the name, the journal a line, and the display says "The PIN was used by <name>" for ten seconds (`pinscreen.recovery_notice`); People and codes shows the notice and "came in with the PIN from a recovery stick" (or "with a recovery code") in the device list (L3: `_public` passes `via`). M2: a code typed over plain http while the owner-only switch bites crossed the network before the refusal; `/api/hello` now carries `owner_https_only` and the https address while the switch bites (nothing else new; a test pins its key set) and the pairing page then shows the link with one sentence why, and no field; the refusal on the server stays as it was, before any check, nothing burnt. L4: the codes leave the page by themselves when it is hidden for more than a moment or after ten minutes, and the words say "not while this screen is shared or projected". L5: a stick already in when pvj-web starts shows nothing when the log's last stick event is under an hour old (the per-hour count lived in memory); out and in again shows it. L6: the drives are read on the stick's own thread (`Recovery.start`, every 3 s) and `poll()` reads what it saw, so no disk IO runs under the PIN screen's lock; the recovery lock has its place in the order (D71) under the PIN screen's. L7: the manual says exactly how to make the file on a Mac, Windows and Linux. Also: the card nudges at two codes or fewer, and CI fetches `b62f203` so the rollback test runs there too. The reviewer agreed with the stick on by default (given M1), eight codes, and not burning a code refused over http. Every redeem test in `RecoveryCodesTest` is a code over http with the switch off (the test box has no certificate).

**Meeting D80 (2026-10-11).** The four recovery routes are the Owner's in D80's table and in `tests/test_roles.py` (never through support), and an Operator gets neither the card nor its switch. A device paired by a code (`via: "recovery"`) or by the stick's PIN (`via: "stick"`) is a full Owner: `Auth.role_of` lowers only `via: "controller"`, and `policy.is_controller` and `held_to_legacy` do not match them (tested). A Guest or Operator device that redeems a code becomes a new Owner device, as intended. `_public` hands out `via` for every device, so D80's "nothing else is handed out" check names it.

Nothing here has run on the box or met a real USB stick as of 2026-10-11; the Mac's tests and CI are the proof so far. Rows in `tools/DEVICE-TESTING.md` say what to try there.

## D82. The box may send its state back over OSC: off by default, to targets the Owner names, under a fixed cap, and never as an answer to a stranger

2026-10-11. Asked "Should the box send its state back to TouchOSC, so the layout shows what is really playing?" the owner answered "Yes, build it" (2026-10-10). Until now `pvj/OSC.md` said "No replies are ever sent": a layout's lights were its own guess, wrong as soon as the panel, a MIDI controller or a second tablet acted. D75 left this as a later owner decision (off unless switched on, allow-listed targets, rate limited). This is that decision.

**The threat model, written before the code.** UDP has no connection and a source address can be forged by any device on the same network. A box that answers is a box that can be made to send packets at someone else. Who can try what:

- *A stranger on the network sends packets with a forged source address, to make the box send at a victim (reflection), or send much more than it was sent (amplification).* So no packet ever decides where the box sends. Feedback goes only to the targets the Owner typed and confirmed in the panel. A packet from an address that is not a listed target causes no packet to anybody, and a packet from a listed target's address causes packets only to that same target, which the Owner wanted sent there anyway.
- *The same stranger floods the box with requests for the full state.* A request sets a flag for its target and nothing more, so a flood of them is one request. What leaves the box is under a hard cap per target and in total, whatever comes in.
- *A wrong or hostile target.* The Owner could type the address of a printer, a router, or a server on the internet, or import a settings file from another building. So a target must be a plain IPv4 address in a private or link-local range, on a subnet the box is directly connected to at the moment of sending, not the subnet's network or broadcast address, not multicast, not loopback, not one of the box's own addresses, and its port must be 1024 or higher (the low ports are where DNS, NTP and SNMP listen: the classic reflectors). The sending socket has a time to live of 1, so even a packet that slipped past these checks could not cross a router.
- *A listed target is another box, or a program that takes OSC commands (Resolume, QLab).* If feedback used the control addresses, the box would drive it, and two boxes that list each other would send in circles. So state has a namespace of its own, `/pvj/state/...`, which means nothing as a command anywhere in this receiver, and the request (`/pvj/state`) is an address the box itself never sends.
- *Somebody listens on the network.* OSC is not encrypted (D78 says so in every place). So nothing secret is ever in a state packet: no PIN, no code, no key, no device name or address, no network detail, no file path. A clip's file name and a shader's name are in it, and anyone on the network can read them.
- *The key (lock 3).* If feedback carried `/k/<key>/`, the key would leave the box in every packet, to whatever sits at a target's address. It never does.
- *An insider below the Owner, or a remote support session.* A new way out of the box is the Owner's to open, at the studio. An Operator, a Guest, a controller and a support session of any role can neither switch it nor change the list.
- *A dead or unroutable target.* Sending must never hold up the player, the panel or the OSC receiver.

**The decisions.**

- **Off by default, and it only sends while OSC itself is on.** Two new keys under `osc`: `feedback_on` (false) and `feedback` (a list of targets). No settings schema change. An older release ignores both and sends nothing: going back fails closed.
- **Who gets it: an explicit list, at most 4 targets, each a name, an IPv4 address and a port.** Checked when saved and again every time something is sent (the box's networks are read again every 10 seconds), so a box that moved to another network, or a file from another box, sends nothing to an address that is no longer local. The outer wall of D78 is not enough here: an extra network the Owner allowed for receiving does not make an address a target.
- **Tied to the locks that are on (the brief left this open).** A target must also be an address that could send right now by the two address locks of D78: with "Only these devices may send" on it must be in that list, and with "A sender must be a paired device" on it must be a paired device's address at that moment (so removing the device, or logging out on it, stops its feedback at once). With neither lock on, the list of targets alone decides. Not chosen: always requiring a paired device, because the paired addresses live in memory only and a restart of the panel would silence every layout until someone opened the panel on the tablet.
- **"Use the device that is sending now" only fills the form.** It puts the address of the last sender that passed every lock into the address field. The Owner still gives the name and the port (a sender's source port is not the port its layout listens on) and presses Add. Nothing is ever added by a packet.
- **Its own route, `POST /api/osc/feedback`: the Owner's, never through remote support** (in `support.REMOTE_DENY` and refused in the handler). `POST /api/osc` ignores these keys. The targets are shown only to an Owner.
- **What is sent: a fixed table of state addresses under `/pvj/state/`** (`pvj/OSC.md` has the table), each with the same tail and the same range as its control address (`/pvj/opacity` 0 to 100 is `/pvj/state/opacity` 0 to 100). Every number is a float32 and every name a string, the two types a TouchOSC control binds directly. Every packet is built from the box's state alone: no byte of a received packet is ever copied into one.
- **From the same state as the controller lights.** The picture of the box the MIDI lights are worked out from moved to `pvj/boxstate.py`, and both read it; the pad lights are `midi.light_state` itself, so a pad lit on a controller is lit in the layout.
- **When: on change, coalesced into one look at the box 5 times a second; a heartbeat every 2 seconds; everything again every 30 seconds; and everything at once on request** (`/pvj/state` from a sender that passed every lock and whose address is a listed target; at most once in 2 seconds per target).
- **The caps, as numbers.** One datagram is at most 1200 bytes. To one target: at most 20 datagrams and 12,000 bytes in any second (15 a second and 9,000 bytes a second, with a burst of 5 and 3,000 on top). To all targets together: at most 60 datagrams and 36,000 bytes in any second (45 and 27,000, with 15 and 9,000 on top). What does not fit waits for the next look and leaves as the newest value only; nothing queues up.
- **The key is never in a state packet**, whether lock 3 is on or off. A layout's control then has one message that sends (`/k/<key>/pvj/opacity`) and one that receives (`/pvj/state/opacity`).
- **Nothing is sent in answer to a refused or malformed packet, ever.** No error replies. That rule of D78 stays.
- **One thread of its own, a socket that never waits.** The receive loop only sets a flag. A send that cannot go (no route, a full buffer) is counted and forgotten. The module's lock is a plain one, never held over a look at the box or a send, and is named in `pvj/locks.py` among those outside the order.

**As built, and what is left open (2026-10-11).**

- `pvj/oscfeedback.py` (the targets, the wire, the caps, the sender), `pvj/boxstate.py` (the lights' picture, moved out of `MidiHub._snapshot` unchanged), `OscManager._apply_feedback`, `_may_be_sent`, `feedback_status`, `Api.osc_feedback`, the card `oscStateCard` in the panel.
- What I tried as an attacker, each a test in `tests/test_osc_feedback.py`: a request from an address that is no target (nothing to anybody); a request from a target's address that fails a lock, the key, the rate limit or the parser (no flag set); 400 packets from 250 forged addresses (every datagram still goes to a listed target); a thousand requests a second for four seconds (one full state per two seconds); everything changing at every look with four targets and a look every millisecond (the caps hold in every one-second window); the box's own packets sent back to it (no command, no request); a target outside the box's networks after a move or from an imported file (nothing sent); the address locks against a target; a full scenario swept for the PIN, the key, a join code, tokens, device names, target names, addresses, the port, a stream's password and a folder.
- **One received packet and what it can cause.** A request of 16 bytes from a target's forged address makes the box send that target one full state, 92 messages in 3 datagrams, about 3,200 bytes today: a ratio of about 200 for that one packet. What bounds it is not the ratio but where it goes and how often: only to a device the Owner listed on the box's own network, at most once in 2 seconds, inside 12,000 bytes a second. A box used as an amplifier against a stranger is what the list prevents; a tablet being sent its own layout's state more often than it asked is the worst that is left.
- **A support session of the Owner's role can still switch OSC itself on and off** (`POST /api/osc`, as before D82), and with it the sending to the Owner's targets stops and starts. It cannot switch feedback, see the targets or change them.
- **A settings file imported by the Owner at the studio brings the switch and the targets** (form checked; where it may really send is decided at every send). A file that says nothing about them leaves the box's own.
- **The plain spelling of an address** (`str(addr) == text`) guards against Python before 3.9.5 reading `192.168.001.020` as an address; on the Python of this Mac the library refuses it first, so breaking that one line fails no test here.
- **Left to the layout: a fader under a finger.** The box sends a level back to the tablet that is moving it, within 0.2 seconds. Whether TouchOSC's fader then jumps under the finger is unknown until a tablet tries; if it does, the layout can ignore incoming values while touched, or the box can hold a level back from the address that just sent it.
- **Not known: whether TouchOSC takes OSC bundles.** The box sends several messages as one bundle (OSC 1.0). If a real TouchOSC ignores bundles, the change is one function (`pack`), and the cap in datagrams must then be raised with care.
- **A look at the box can wait for the player** (one status call, at most every 0.4 seconds, as the lights make). While it waits the heartbeat waits too, which is why the docs say 6 seconds and not 2 before "not answering".

**After the independent security review of 5dd0e64 (2026-10-11): nothing High; one Medium and three Lows fixed, each with a test seen to fail first.**

- *Medium: a support session of the Owner's role could read the targets after all*, in two files the first build did not think of: the settings export without passwords and the diagnostics file both carried the `osc` section whole. Now `feedback_on` and `feedback` are taken out of an export asked for by a support session (by the device or by the tunnel's address) and out of every diagnostics file, whoever asks, because that file leaves the box. The Owner's own export at the studio still holds them, as settings. An import of a file without them leaves the box's own.
- *The same look at the other new keys of recent decisions.* Still readable by a support session of the Owner's role in an export and by anyone who is handed a diagnostics file, and left as they are (each is another decision's, and none was changed without its own test): `osc.only` (the single addresses of lock 1, D78, which says the rest of the OSC page stays readable for support), `osc.allow`, `osc.paired_roles` and `paired_hours`; the projectors' addresses and ports; the names and roles of paired devices and the support log in a diagnostics file. Not in either: the OSC key (its own section), the recovery codes (D81: the switch and a count only), the PIN, tokens, guest controls and the controller code (never exported; the diagnostics file says only what the controller code means), the secure connection's key (D79, not a settings section).
- *Low: a file name with a broken byte, or a number beyond a float32, raised in the encoder and silenced the sender until the clip changed.* A lone surrogate is sent as "?", numbers are held to plus or minus 3.0e38, and a message that still cannot be built is left out, said once in the journal, and the rest of the state leaves.
- *Low: "directly connected" counted every interface but two.* The box's own networks are now those of its real ports (wired, and Wi-Fi with its hotspot: NetworkManager runs the hotspot on `wlan0` itself). Ports whose name starts with `lo`, `wg`, `tun`, `tap`, `tailscale`, `zt`, `docker`, `veth`, `br-` or `virbr` are never counted.
- *Low: a target that went stale with a move of the box stood in the way of adding a new one.* Only the target being added is checked against the networks; a stale one stays in the list, is sent nothing, says "Not on this network now, nothing is sent to it" on the page at once, and has Remove.
- *The four questions, answered by the reviewer and adopted:* the fader's own value coming back is left to the layout (a script ignores received values while the control is touched); bundles stay, and whether TouchOSC takes them is to be confirmed on the tablet; the lowest port stays 1024; targets follow the locks that are on.
- The effect's amount and a running Vibes are in a test now.

Nothing here has met a real tablet, a real TouchOSC or the box as of 2026-10-11; the tests on a Mac and CI are the proof so far. Rows in `tools/DEVICE-TESTING.md` say what to try.

## D83. OSC: the paired-device lock comes on with the first switch-on, an open box says so, and the state names the eight controls

2026-10-11. Asked about OSC's defaults the owner said "Yes, proceed with the OSC recommendations", and about the state the box sends (D82): "the shader control names should match those from the shader that's playing, same for effects."

**What was wrong.** OSC has no login. With OSC on and the three locks of D78 off, anyone on the network can play, stop, black out and move the picture; the roles and the guest lock of D80 do not apply to OSC. D78 left every lock off so that an update changed nothing, which is right for a box that already runs and wrong for a box that is new.

**The decisions.**

- **The first time OSC is switched on, on a box where it never was on, "A sender must be a paired device" comes on with it.** The request that switches OSC on may say what it wants for that lock and is obeyed; the panel's switch says nothing, so it gets the lock. Another lock chosen in the same request does not stand in for it.
- **How "never" is known, with no schema change: one new key under `osc`, `ever_on`.** New boxes have it false from the default settings. On a box that updates it is written once, the first time this code sets the receiver up (`OscManager._note_ever_on`): true when OSC is on, or when anything about OSC is not as a new box has it (the port, an extra network, any lock or state key, a key in either place); false only for an untouched section. From the first switch-on it is true and nothing lowers it: not switching off, not an import. The traces are read only at that first start; afterwards only the mark counts, so changing the port on a new box before the first switch-on does not lose the lock.
- **A box where OSC is on, or was on before, keeps its settings exactly.** An update must never lock out a working tablet. Tested with a settings file written by master's own code with OSC on and every lock off: it stays open, the tablet's packet is still taken, and switching OSC off and on again adds no lock.
- **The one case that cannot be told apart, said plainly.** A box where OSC was on under an older release, was switched off again, and where nothing else about OSC was ever changed, has the same file as a new box. Its next switch-on brings the lock. That is not an update locking a tablet out (OSC is off, nothing works at that moment), the owner is at the panel pressing the switch, and the page says the lock came on and how to switch it off.
- **What the lock asks is said where the switch is pressed.** A message at the switch (on Setup's index and on the OSC page: `oscStep`), and a note beside the lock for as long as it is on by the box's choice (`paired_default`, a second key an older release ignores; the owner's first word about the lock removes it): the tablet must open the panel and pair once, as an Owner device unless "Owner and operator devices" is chosen, and again if its address changes.
- **A settings file.** An export carries neither key (they are this box's history, not settings). An import never lowers the mark. A file that has OSC on comes as it is, with its own locks: no lock is added to the owner's own settings from a working box, and the warning below then says what is open. Not chosen: adding the lock on import, which would silently change a restored show box. Confirmed on 2026-10-11 when the question was put (the owner was away and had asked the coordinator to decide low and medium risk questions on advice): as built, because an import restores what the owner saved, and the warning shows if it is open. Likewise confirmed: a Guest is not told that OSC is open.
- **A factory reset makes the box new again**: the next switch-on brings the lock.
- **While OSC is on with all three locks off, the panel says so in three places**: a plain line on Setup > OSC, a short one on Setup's index row for OSC ("Check"), and a row on the health page: "OSC is open: anyone on this network can control the box. Switch on a lock." The Owner sees all three. An Operator sees it on the health page (and through it on the Health row of Setup), with "The owner can switch on a lock", and none of the settings he may not change: the OSC page is the Owner's as before. A Guest is not told: `GET /api/health` leaves it out for him. (The three switches themselves were already readable by every paired device in `GET /api/osc`, D78; that is unchanged.)
- **The words of the warning are the box's own** (`osc.OPEN_TEXT`), so the three places cannot drift apart.
- **The docs say what each lock stops and does not stop**, as a short table in `pvj/OSC.md` and `docs/MANUAL.md`: a sender's address can be forged on the same network; the key travels in the clear and can be read on the same Wi-Fi; none of it is encryption; on a shared venue network the box and the tablet belong on a network of their own. For a live night: paired devices plus a key.

**The names in the state (the second part).**

- **New addresses, all strings but the values:** `/pvj/state/shader/control/<n>/name` and `/kind`, `/pvj/state/effect/control/<n>` (f 0 to 1), `/pvj/state/effect/control/<n>/name` and `/kind`, n = 1 to 8. The effect's amount was in the state already; its eight control values were not, and are now.
- **The name is what the panel shows.** The panel labels a control `i.label || i.name` (`shaders.js`, `effects.js`), and the parser already puts the file's LABEL, or its NAME, into `label`. That is sent. An effect file's own input called "amount" is sent as "Amount (the filter's own)", as the panel says it.
- **The count is the knobs' own** (`engine.nth_control`, the count `/pvj/shader/control/<n>` and a MIDI knob use): numbers that can move, switches, choices and events, in the file's order, the first eight. So a colour and a point never have a place among the eight, and the kind is one of `float`, `bool`, `choice` (a long with VALUES), `int` (a long over a range), `event`, or empty for a place with no control. The brief named "colour" as a kind; there is none to send, because no knob drives one.
- **Cleaned as a clip's name is**, and shorter: no control characters, at most 40 characters (`MAX_NAME`; the parser allows 60, a knob's label in a layout does not need them), encoded with replace.
- **Inside D82's caps, measured.** Names and kinds leave when they change (the sender only sends what differs from what a target was last sent) and with every full state, never at every look. A full state was 92 messages in 3 datagrams, about 3,200 bytes; it is now 132 messages in 5 datagrams, 5,124 bytes, and is out 0.4 seconds after it starts (the byte cap spreads it over three looks). With the worst names a file could give (sixteen names of 40 four-byte characters) it is 7 datagrams and 7,740 bytes, out in 0.6 seconds. Every datagram stays under 1200 bytes and every second under 20 datagrams and 12,000 bytes to one target. The caps were not raised.
- **One received packet now causes more.** A request of 16 bytes from a listed target's forged address makes the box send that target about 5,100 bytes (up to 7,740), where D82 said about 3,200. The bound is the same as before: only to a listed target, once in 2 seconds, under the cap.
- **Nothing secret.** A control's name comes from a shader file on the box; like a clip's name, anyone on the network can read it.
- **One cache of parsed inputs per engine.** A shader and an effect can be on together (D74); one shared cache would have read and parsed two files five times a second.

**After the independent review of #124 at 8f8fb8a (2026-10-11): safe to merge and deploy after these; each fixed with a test seen to fail first.**

- *M1: a save that failed at the first start stopped the receiver.* The mark is written when the receiver is set up; on a full or read-only disk the `OSError` escaped `apply()` and nothing listened, where master writes nothing there. The save is now tried and, if it fails, said once and tried again the next time the receiver is set up; the receiver always starts.
- *M2: an import lost the first switch-on lock.* A new box, the port changed, its own export imported, then switched on: the import read the traces again (`used_before`) and marked the box "was on", so no lock came. It asks `never_on` now (only the mark counts once it is written) and always writes the mark, so nothing the file brings is ever read as a trace. That was the one place besides the first start that read the traces.
- *L1: a Guest was told after all.* `GET /api/osc` gave every paired device `open` and the words. Below Operator they are left out. (The three switches a Guest could read before are as they were, D78.)
- *L2: a full state could starve under heavy change.* With 40 values changing at every look, four targets and the longest names, changed values took the budget first and the shared cap followed the list's order: the fourth target had no full state after 9 seconds in the test (20 in the review). What a target was never sent now goes before what it has an older value of, and the target that goes first changes with every look. In the test every target is complete within 5 seconds and the changing values still arrive; no cap was raised.
- *L3:* CI's unit step prints its 25 slowest tests (on Python 3.12, which has `--durations`); the limit stays at twenty-five minutes.
- *L4: the health summary said ok beside a row that said Check.* Decided: **an open OSC is a "check", not a failure.** `overall` is `warn` when OSC is open unless something is really wrong (`bad` stays `bad`); a Guest's summary, like his page, does not change.

**Not done, not known.** Nothing here has run on the box or a tablet. Whether TouchOSC shows a string that arrives in a bundle in a label is unknown (D82's open question about bundles). The browser test was written without being run on the development Mac (no Playwright there); CI runs it. No new control was added to the panel (the warning is words), so the inventory of controls has no new entry.

## D84. Remote support: a helper with the Owner's role may do anything that ends with the session, and nothing that outlives it or locks the owner out

2026-10-11. The owner asked what an Owner may do from a remote support session and suggested "everything since it is a 'phone for help' solution". He was advised "nearly everything", and said: "proceed with ... your recommendations for the remote sessions". (D83 is taken by the branch `osc-defaults`, open at the same time.)

**The principle.** A helper in a remote session with the Owner's role may do anything that ends when the session ends, and nothing that outlives it or locks the owner out.

**The fact that frames it** (corrected after the security review: the first wording, "run any code on the box", overstated it). A remote session of the Owner's role can already upload a software update and start it, and the update must be signed by a key this box trusts: `pvj-update` checks the signature against `/etc/pvj/allowed_signers` as root before anything is installed, and no route of the panel changes the signers. So a helper can put any release of ours on the box, an older one too, but not a program of his own. The refusals that remain are not about limiting a trusted helper in the moment. They are about persistence (a way in that is still there after Stop) and lock-out (the owner's own way in), they stop an honest helper from leaving something behind by accident, and with the log they make a session auditable. They are not a wall against a dishonest helper who was given "Everything", who can change every setting an owner can; the docs say so.

**Opened to a remote session of the Owner's role** (taken out of `support.REMOTE_DENY`; each has its row in `tests/test_roles.py` and is asked with its real handler in `tests/test_support_reach.py`):

- **Settings import** (`POST /api/system/settings/import`), without what stays refused below. What a file can carry was read section by section: pads, modules, theme (and added themes), mix, OSC, schedule, streams, control (DMX and MIDI), autostart, audio, overlay, projectors, mapper, sync, shaders, room, recovery. Every one but the last is set by a route a remote Owner session may use anyway. **Recovery** (the stick switch, D81) is set under `/api/recovery`, refused to every session: for a helper that part of the file is dropped and the box's own stays (`boxcare.REMOTE_KEPT`; the answer and the log say so). **Devices, the PIN, join codes, the controller code choice, the guest lock, remote support's own settings and log, the secure connection and the OSC key are no section of any settings file**: a file that names one is refused whole, for everybody (tested with each). A test holds every section to a row (`boxcare.REMOTE_SETS`), so a new section cannot slip in unthought.
- **Theme add and remove** (`/api/theme/add`, `/api/theme/remove`). A theme is checked data: unknown keys, anything that is not a colour or a listed value, a name with markup, a path in the id are refused by the same check as at the studio. Only an added theme can be removed; the box's own look comes back.
- **The OSC key** (`/api/osc/key`): show and renew, under the same limit of shows as a device at the studio. **A helper who saw or made the key knows it.** The session's log says so, and after the session the Remote support page and its row in Setup tell the owner to make a new key; the word goes away when a new key is made at the studio (`SupportManager.key_renewed`).
- **The OSC state targets** (`/api/osc/feedback`). A target is still only a private address on a subnet of the box's own real ports, checked when added and at every send: the tunnel's network never counts (D82), so a helper cannot aim the box's state at himself or out of the building.
- **Consistent with that, the stripping of D82 is undone for him only where he can read the same thing anyway:** `GET /api/osc` shows a remote Owner session the targets, and its settings export holds them (a session that reaches the export has the Owner's role). **Not undone:** the diagnostics file holds no targets for anybody, because that file leaves the box; an export with passwords stays refused through the tunnel; the key is in no export for anybody.
- **The guest lock** (`/api/guests`): a switch an Operator or the owner sets back.
- **Removing a device** (`/api/devices/revoke`): a Guest's or an Operator's only. Never an Owner's device (403), which would lock the owner out. Only a device in the list of paired devices can be named, so never his own session, another support login, a controller or one of the box's own callers (404). The role is read from the list at that moment, not from the request.

**Kept refused for every remote session, whatever its role** (`support.REMOTE_DENY` and `REMOTE_DENY_PREFIX_WHY`, each with its reason beside it; `REFUSED` in the test is the same list written by hand):

- pairing and panel sessions (`/api/pair`, `/api/session`) and invitations (`/api/devices/invite`): a device paired now stays paired after Stop;
- everything under `/api/access`: codes, links, what the display shows, the controller code choice;
- the PIN (show, rotate, unlock) and the QR code;
- everything under `/api/recovery` and `/api/https`: the owner's ways back in, and the switch that could shut his devices out;
- the support session's own settings, start and extend (`/api/support/config`, `/start`, `/extend`): a helper does not lengthen or widen his own access;
- power-off: nobody remote can switch the box back on (a restart stays allowed; it ends the session);
- factory reset: it removes every device, the owner's too;
- **added by this decision, not in the brief: the confirm of a network change** (`POST /api/network/confirm`). A network change stays pending and goes back by itself unless it is confirmed; the confirm is the proof that whoever pressed it still reaches the box. A helper in the tunnel reaching it proves nothing about the studio, so his confirm could leave the owner's devices unable to reach the panel. He may still make the change and take it back; someone at the studio confirms it. The owner is asked whether to keep this.

**Remote sessions of roles below Owner are unchanged**: held to the old presenter list (`policy.LEGACY_LIVE`, D80). The table test holds that for every route.

**Everything a remote session changes is in the support log.** `SupportManager.note` writes into the session's own entry of `support_log` (`did`: the time and what was done, never a value: no key, no address, no file content, no request body) and into the journal. The seven opened routes say what was done in their own words ("removed the guest device ...", "imported a settings file; left as the box has it: recovery; taken: ..."). **Every other POST a session is answered is there by its route** ("used /api/osc", "used /api/network", "uploaded a software update"), written by `Api.handle` and the server's own upload paths: a test walks the table of every route, so a new route is covered by itself, and the few POSTs that change nothing are named in `support.NOT_A_CHANGE`. The first build logged only the opened routes while the page said "everything"; the advisor's review caught it. **The list cannot be flushed** (as first built; the security review below found that it could, and it is two lists now): the same thing again is one line with a count (kept in memory until the next save, so a fader dragged through the tunnel does not write the settings file ten times a second), and when 40 lines are full the first lines stay and the rest are counted. The owner reads it under Advanced, Recent sessions.

**The handlers hold by themselves.** Each opened handler asks `Api._remote_owner`: a request from the tunnel, or one that carries a support login, passes only as a support login of the Owner's role through the tunnel of a session that is open now. A login that outlived its session, a login used from the studio's network, and a studio device's token used through the tunnel are refused there too, whatever the route table says.

**What I tried as an attacker** (each a test in `tests/test_support_reach.py`):

- *A way in left behind.* A file that adds a device, a PIN hash, a join code, the controller code choice, support settings or a longer session limit, the guest lock, an HTTPS switch or an OSC key: refused whole, in the settings part and beside it. A file that switches the recovery stick: dropped for him. A theme that is more than a theme (an extra `css` or `script` key, `url(...)`, `</style><script>`, `expression(...)`, `@import`, markup in the name, a path in the id, a 2 MB file): refused. State targets pointed at the tunnel, at his own address outside, at the box itself, at a broadcast address, at a port below 1024: refused. Extending, restarting or widening his own session, inviting or pairing a device, a code, the PIN, recovery, the secure connection: refused for a session of each role. After Stop: his login is dead on every opened route and in the handlers themselves, no device and no code was added, and his token is nowhere in the settings.
- *Locking the owner out.* Removing an Owner's device (403, also the second Owner's, also a device whose role was raised in the list meanwhile); removing himself or a system identity (404); power-off and factory reset (403); an import leaves the PIN, every device, the support settings and the running session as they were.
- *Two more ways to shut the owner out.* The clock set far forward: only a device that joined with a guest code and went unused for a week is dropped by time; an Owner's and an Operator's never are. The network: see the confirm above, now refused.
- **Found, and closed the same day by decision (below):** the networks OSC accepts from, and a MIDI control that shows an access code. **Left as they are, said plainly:** (1) *The key he saw.* Nothing takes the knowledge back; only a new key ends it, and the panel says so. (2) *Backups.* Each import keeps a copy of the settings before it and only the last three are kept, so three imports by a helper push the owner's older copies out.

**In the panel.** Before the owner starts a session, the Remote support page says in two short lists what a helper can and cannot do (`supportReach`). After a session it says that support changed things and where the list is, lists them under Recent sessions, and, if support saw the key in any past session, says to make a new one until a new key is made at the studio.

**Not done, not known.** Nothing here ran through a real tunnel or on a box. The browser step was written without being run on the development Mac (no Playwright there); CI runs it. An independent security review comes before any merge.

**Decided on the questions the first build left (2026-10-11, the same day).** The owner was away and had asked the coordinator to decide low and medium risk questions on advice; each was decided by this decision's own principle, that nothing a helper does may outlive the session as a way in.

| Question | Decided | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| The confirm of a network change through the tunnel | Kept refused, as built | A network change is confirmed by someone who can walk to the box if it goes wrong |
| The networks OSC accepts from | A remote Owner session may not add or widen one beyond a private or link-local range the box is directly connected to (the rule a state target is held to, D82); removing and narrowing are allowed | An outside network accepted for OSC would be a way in that outlives the session |
| A MIDI control that shows an access code | Refused to every remote session whatever its role: never added, never changed; one that exists may be removed | The rule that stops an Operator mapping owner-level actions (D80), applied to every remote session |

- **The networks, as built.** `Api.remote_allow_refused`: a network in the new list passes if it is one the box already accepts from or lies inside one (unchanged or narrowed), or if it is IPv4, private or link-local, and inside a subnet of one of the box's own real ports now (the tunnel's network never counts). Anything else is refused with 403 on `POST /api/osc`, also what he removed a moment before. By a file, the same check: if the file's list would add such a network, the box's own list stays and the answer and the log say "osc.allow"; the rest of the file comes. At the studio the Owner adds what he likes, as before.
- **The code controls, as built.** In `Api.midi_map` a support session is held to the Operator's rule (no mapping with such an action added or changed, no mapping put in front of one, no control of a standard layout set to one, no reset that would put a layout's code control back), with the one difference that it may remove such a mapping, alone or with Clear. `Api.set_midi` holds it to the Operator's rule for switching a standard layout that has such a control (refused both ways, which is stricter than "may remove" for that one case). By a file: a code mapping that is not exactly one the box has is left out, which standard layouts are on stays as the box has it, and the answer and the log say so. The switch that lets a controller show a code at all is under `/api/access` and was refused before.
- Tests: `test_no_network_from_outside_is_added_to_those_osc_accepts_from` and `test_a_control_that_shows_an_access_code_is_never_set_or_changed_by_a_session` in `tests/test_support_reach.py`.
- **The OSC key line, confirmed.** A session that is shown the key or makes a new one marks its log entry; once that session is over, the Remote support page (`#supportoldkey`) and its row in Setup say to make a new key, for any past session and not only the last, until a new key is made at the studio. The server side is tested (`test_the_key_he_saw_is_said_and_the_owner_is_told_to_make_a_new_one`); the line's appearing in a browser is not, because the browser harness has no support helper to run a session with.

**After the critical security review of #125 at 0c06cec (2026-10-11): "not yet; safe to merge after these". Each fixed with a test seen to fail first.**

- *M1: the session log's cap hid the telling lines.* One list of forty: a remote Owner has some sixty POST routes that each wrote a distinct `used /api/...` line, so 45 calls and then an update left the update as a count. Now two lists in a session's entry. **`major`**: what the opened routes changed, a software update or a settings file brought in, a device removed, and everything the session was refused; never capped or displaced by routine lines, the first 200 kept, and if even that fills, `major_more` counts the rest and the page says the list is full in a line of its own. **`did`**: routine use, one line per route with a count. The panel shows the major lines first.
- *M2: refused attempts were nowhere.* A support login refused a route no session may use (`REMOTE_DENY` and the prefixes) now leaves "was refused POST /api/pair" as a major line, written in the gate, so the paths the server answers itself are covered; so do the rules added by decision (a network for OSC, a MIDI control that shows an access code, a standard layout that has one) and a try at an Owner's device. A request through the tunnel with no login at all is said too ("not signed in"). The same refusal again is a count: neither the settings file nor the journal is written a second time, so a flood of refused requests costs one line. A refusal by role (a session below Owner asking for an Owner's route) is not a line: it is what that session is.
- *L1: the guest lock's handler did not ask for itself.* `set_guests` asks `_remote_owner` like the other six, and a test drives every handler of `REMOTE_OWNER_OPENED` directly with a login whose session is over, through the tunnel's address and the studio's.
- *L2: the framing, and which update.* Above. And the log names what came in: an update's file name and sha256 ("uploaded a software update: pvj-1.2.3.tar.gz, sha256 ..."), an imported settings file's sha256 and the sections it really changed ("imported a settings file, sha256 ...; changed: osc, schedule; left as the box has it: recovery"). Neither is a secret.
- *L3: the clock route is open to a remote Owner session, and that is accepted.* `POST /api/system/clock` only works while the clock is not set from the network, and takes a time up to 2036. **What it cannot move:** the session's own end, join codes, the controller code, PIN and code lockouts and the limits on showing the PIN or the key all run on a monotonic clock (`SupportManager._clock`, the helper's own timer, `Auth._clock`), so a helper cannot lengthen his session or a code, or shorten a lockout, with it. **What it does move:** when the schedule's entries run; the times written into the journal, the support log and the recovery events (a helper could make his session's lines carry a wrong time, and the route's use is itself a line in the log); and the week after which an unused device that joined with a guest code is dropped (an Owner's and an Operator's device never are).
- **Accepted persistence, named.** A helper with the Owner's role can point the box at other machines, and that stays after the session: Boxes in step (which server this box follows), projector addresses, streams, and what the schedule and power-up play. These are settings an owner changes every day, each is a line in the session's log, none of them is a way into the panel, and refusing them would make remote help useless. The page says so before Start and tells the owner to read the list afterwards.
- **A copy of the settings from before the session that no session can push out.** Each import kept a copy of the settings before it and only the last three were kept, so three imports by a helper pushed the owner's own copies out. Decided: a session's copies are kept apart and never remove one of the owner's. The first import of a session keeps the settings as they were before that session touched them by a file (`settings.json.before-support-session-<id>-<time>`): one per session, the last three sessions kept, so only a new session, which someone at the studio starts, can push one out. Its later imports turn over among themselves (`.before-support-import-<time>`, the last three). At most six more files; a factory reset removes them with the others. Not done: a copy at the start of every session whether or not it imports (changes made route by route are in the log, not in a copy).

**After the re-check of 181e614 (2026-10-11): one gap left in M1, closed.** The major list could still be filled: 110 themes added and removed with distinct ids made 220 distinct lines, and the update after them was a count again. Now three lists, and the rule that no sequence of actions keeps an update, a settings file or a removed device out of what the owner sees:

- **`always`**: a software update (name and sha256), an imported settings file (sha256, what it changed), a device removed. Nothing else writes to this list. Each is rare and heavy; it holds 100, and past that the first 50 and the newest 50 stay and `always_dropped` says how many lie between. The panel shows it first.
- **`major`**: what the other opened routes changed and what the session was refused. Every such line's text is fixed by the code; what a request chooses goes into a detail, of which the first five are kept beside the count ("added a theme: t0, t1, t2, t3, t4 and 105 others (110 times)"). So the number of distinct major lines is bounded by the code, not by the helper, and is well under the 200 the list holds. Every caller was gone through, and a test reads the source: a call that writes a major line either is in the never-capped class or has no part in its text that a request chooses. A refusal names a route of the route table; a path nobody answers is a 404 and no line.
- **`did`**: routine use by route; an uploaded clip is one counted line with a few of the names.
- Tested with the reviewer's sequence (110 theme cycles, an update, an import, a revoke): all three are in the session's entry and in what the panel is given.


**NDI (D85, merged to master while this was in review): what a support session may do with it (2026-10-11).**

| Route or part | A session of the Owner's role | Below it | Why |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `GET` and `POST /api/ndi/discovery` (the discovery server on this box) | refused | refused | D85's own rule, kept: a program that listens on the network is the owner's, in the room |
| `POST /api/modules/ndi-discovery` (its switch, on or off) | refused | refused | the same |
| `GET /api/ndi`, `POST /api/ndi` (the input: addresses, discovery servers to ask, groups, sound, sources) | as the Owner | as before (D80) | follows Streams |
| `POST /api/modules/inputs-ndi` to **on** | refused | refused | decided here, on the coordinator's lean: switching the NDI input on makes the box listen to senders on the network and run NDI's library, and stays on after the session |
| `POST /api/modules/inputs-ndi` to **off** | allowed | as before | it takes something away |
| a settings file: `modules` switching `inputs-ndi` or `ndi-discovery` on | left off, the rest of the file comes | no import | a file does no more than the routes; for the owner in the room an import is as it was |
| a settings file: `ndi.addresses` and `ndi.discovery` with an entry the box does not have and that is not on one of the box's own networks now | that list is left as the box has it | no import | no pointing the input at a machine off the box's networks by a file; the tunnel's network never counts |
| a settings file: `ndi.own_discovery` switched on | left as the box has it | no import | it leans on the discovery server no session may switch |
| `ndi_discovery` (the server's own settings) | no part of any settings file (D85, `boxcare.NEVER`) | | |

One thing is not symmetric and is said plainly: by the route (`POST /api/ndi`) a remote Owner session can add any private address or discovery server, as an Owner can and as for a stream's address; only a file is held to the box's own networks. Both leave a line in the session's log. `support.REMOTE_NO_MODULE_ON` holds the two module ids with their reasons; `Api.set_module` and `BoxCare._remote_parts` read it. Tests: `NdiTest` in `tests/test_support_reach.py`.

## D85. The box can run NDI's Discovery Server: placed by root from the owner's copy, run by a supervisor under its own account, switched by the Owner in the room

The owner's words (2026-10-11): "let's proceed with option 2 and build the discovery server and bake into the box." (D83 and D84 are held by the branches `osc-defaults` and `support-reach`.)

Why: D62's groups and discovery settings point the box at a discovery server; a venue needs one on a machine that is always on, and the box is that machine. NDI's Discovery Server is closed source, listens on the network, and its licence forbids handing it on. So: **never bundled or downloaded**; root's command `pvj-ndi-runtime server install <folder or file>` copies it from the owner's copy after checking the copy (no link followed, a size cap, an ELF program of this processor, root's) and prints its sha256; nothing is executed at install. **A command of its own**, not folded into the runtime's install: a listener is its own decision, and a box may have it without the NDI input. **Its own account** (`pvj-ndisc`) and unit, with every sandbox line the NDI helper has and fewer address families, less memory, and the box's and the helper's folders out of sight. **The unit runs this project's supervisor, not NDI's program**: the panel is not root and must not be able to start or stop a unit, so the supervisor is always there, idle, answers only the panel's account, and starts NDI's program with `-service` (as NDI's own unit does), `-bind` and `-port` (each checked by the supervisor itself) only while the module is on, and ends a program that says it listens on another address than the one chosen; it restarts a program that ends, with a growing pause. **Bound to one network's address** (the wired one by default), never to all unless the Owner chooses it; the panel looks again every ten seconds and has the server started again when the address changed; a network without an address stops the server and it never moves to another by itself. **The Owner's alone, and never through remote support**, the module's switch included (`REMOTE_DENY`): a program listening on the network is opened by someone in the room. **"Use this box's own discovery server"** on NDI input is a switch, not a saved address, so it follows the server. **No count of registrations**: NDI documents no way to ask. `ndi_discovery` is never exported or imported. No schema change; master has no such module and keeps the keys.

Rejected: bundling the program (the licence); the panel starting a unit through the root helper (a new root action reachable from the panel, where a socket and an idle supervisor need none); DynamicUser (the panel could not reach its socket); binding to every address by default; following the address to another network when the chosen one loses its own; reading registrations out of the server's private protocol.

Cost and what is not known: a second closed program on the box; an idle Python supervisor on boxes that set it up; a changed address means telling every sender again. **NDI's program has never been run by this project**: that it starts under this sandbox, that its arguments behave as documented, and everything a real network shows are device steps N27 to N30. See [pvj/NDI.md](../pvj/NDI.md) and SECURITY.md.

## D86. NDI with sound: the player's buffering pause is off for that load, and a picture that stands is written once more (2026-10-12)

A source whose picture stands while its sound goes on (one test picture and a tone; a paused video) is played like this: the Matroska stream is loaded with mpv's `cache-pause=no`, and the helper writes a picture that no other has followed for half a second once more (the stream's first picture at once, a millisecond after itself).

Added the same day, after the box's own player opened its sound device and played nothing: the load also has `initial-audio-sync=no`. mpv does not begin the sound until the picture has reached the first sound's time, and a picture that stands never does.

Why both. mpv takes a picture track that has run dry for a slow network and pauses everything until a picture comes: no sound for as long as the picture stands, and afterwards everything as late as it stood, for good. That is a player's rule, and it is the player's option that ends it. The second picture is for two other things: mpv 0.38 and later do not finish opening a stream until its picture track has two blocks with different times, and every mpv shows a picture only once it has the one after it.

Added the same day for a moving source (the owner: picture and sound must be in step and stay so): the stream is made whole instead of asking the player to mend it. Its time 0 is when its first picture is through the pipe; the sound track begins at 0 (the first block cut, or silence up to it); and silence is written as time passes when the sound stops, so its times never jump (mpv starts both tracks again at a jump of 5 seconds). `initial-audio-sync=no` stays, because a still's sound must never wait for a picture. Not done, and the open question for sync: the stream's times are arrival times; the sender's own timestamps are not used.

Rejected: writing the still again and again at a slow rate and leaving the pause on (measured: it plays, but about as late as the gap between the repeats, and it costs 4 MB a time for as long as the picture stands); a rule in the panel that waits for mpv's clock to move (while a picture stands, mpv's `time-pos` stands by design).

Cost and what is not known: one more buffer of the frame's size in the helper, and one more frame written per still. Measured on the test Pi with mpv 0.40 run by hand, without a screen or a sound device; the box's own player has not shown it yet, and mpv 0.37 only through CI. See [pvj/NDI.md](../pvj/NDI.md).

## D87. One sound source for the whole box: an allowed NDI source whose sound is played under every NDI picture, on a connection of the helper's own (2026-10-12)

The owner: "I have VLC sending audio through NDI Audio instead of through the system device ... I'd like the NDI page on mastercontrol to account for networked audio such as NDI Audio or Dante." His picture comes from NDI Scan Converter, whose size changes at every clip, and his sound from a separate sender that has sound and no picture. Such a sender could not be played at all: the helper waits for a first picture.

Decided, step 1 of the design agreed with him: the Owner may name ONE source as where the sound comes from. Its sound is played under whatever NDI picture is on the screen.

- **The helper.** The sound's connection belongs to the service, not to the picture's receiver (`SoundFeed` in `pvj/ndi.py`): a picture that changes size is closed and opened again, and the sound's connection stays. It is opened for sound alone (the library's audio-only bandwidth). The picture's own connection is then never asked for sound. The feed's thread hands each block to the receiver that is attached (`Receiver.take_audio`), which checks it and gives it its time exactly as it does a picture's own sound; the Matroska writer and the timing rules of D86 are unchanged.
- **When it is connected.** With the first picture that needs it, and then kept. Closed when the panel says nothing of NDI is on the screen, when the input or sound is switched off, when another source is chosen, and by itself after 30 seconds without a picture to play under. Never while nothing of NDI plays: the box holds no connection it has no use for.
- **Away, quiet, lost.** Away when a picture is opened: the picture plays as bare frames; the helper looks again every two seconds, and the first block of sound ends the picture as "changed", which the panel answers by opening it again with sound (the road late sound always took). Quiet at that moment, after it has had sound before: the new stream has a sound track from the start, in the form the last sound had, and silence as time passes; so a clip that begins before its sound does is not opened twice. Lost while it plays: silence is written and the picture goes on.
- **Settings, no new schema.** `ndi.sound_from` is the id of one of the allowed sources (the list pads use), never a name or an address; `ndi.sound_delay_ms` is a whole number from -250 to 500. The three choices the page is to offer are two keys: sound off; sound on and no id (each picture's own sound); sound on and an id.
